===PAGE 3=== (ocr, 23 lines)
   1|               Emily M. (Moore) Foster, 96, a longtime Lunenburg resident, passed away peacefully on June 28, 2021.
   2|              From Cub Scout Den Mother to beloved Children's Librarian to Meals on Wheels volunteer to busy Senior
   3|              Center member, Emily was active in the Lunenburg community, and enjoyed golf and bowling leagues and
   4|              the "Bookworms" club. Emily loved her "Story Hour" kids from her 20+ years as the Children's Librarian
   5|                  of the Ritter Memorial Library in Lunenburg. After retirement, she became an active member of the
   6|             Lunenburg Friendly Seniors and the Eagle House Senior Center. She also worked for years as a poll worker
   7|                      for elections in Lunenburg and assisted in the yard sales for the United Parish of Lunenburg.
   8|                                                  Robert M. Herrick, "Mickey" Herrick. 73, passed away on May 9th,
   9|                                                  2021, surrounded by his immediate family. He was a graduate of
  10|                                                   Lunenburg High School during which time he was Vice President of his
  11|                                                  class and an accomplished athlete. Mickey continued his love of sports
  12|                                                  having played men's softball on numerous local teams throughout his life.
  13|                                                  He truly cherished the lifelong friendships and memories that were made
  14|                                                   while he was playing. Mickey was a prominent figure in the community
  15|                                                   and a local business owner for over 45 years. He was most known for
  16|                                                  "Mickey's Place,"   ', a Lunenburg staple which served wonderful food and
  17|                                                  spirits for over 28 years. When not cracking jokes across the bar, Mickey
  18|                                                   could be found coaching T-ball, Little League and LS Varsity Softball,
  19|                                                  and serving on the Trustee Board over the years. He was a passionate
  20|             sports fan and loved spending his free time falling asleep in his chair watching a Boston Red Sox or New
  21|             England Patriots game, Howard Stern, or anything political. More than anything, Mickey loved to spend
  22|             time with his wife, Kathleen, children and grandchildren.
  23|                                                                           2

===PAGE 6=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                       ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   2|                                        ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   3|                                                        AREA-26.63 SQUARE MILES
   4|                                                         ROAD MILES-ABOUT 100
   5|             Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4
   6|             square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts approximately 43 miles from Boston. It
   7|             is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of
   8|             Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by the Town of
   9|             Townsend.
  10|             Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters in the town.
  11|             Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are generally administered
  12|             by a five member Select Board and a Town Manager. Local school affairs are administered by an elected
  13|             seven member School Committee while local taxes are assessed by a three member Board of Assessors, all
  14|             are appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms.
  15|             Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies account
  16|             for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development. Lake Whalom and the former
  17|             Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This district
  18|             was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster
  19|             and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect characteristics of
  20|             these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  21|             developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. The third body of water is
  22|             Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been
  23|             modified to year-round residences.
  24|             Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major
  25|             arteries (Routes 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's
  26|             and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into
  27|             single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg. In 2013 the Annual Town Meeting voted to
  28|             establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve and enhance farming in the community and to
  29|             administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw.
  30|             The 2015 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District") bylaw to
  31|             regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" structures as defined by
  32|               he Village District Bylaw.
  33|             residential development. While the Village District Bylaw encourages the economic vitalization of
  34|             downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District Bylaw ensures that it will not come at the
  35|             expense of the town's historical character and integrity.
  36|                                                                          5

===PAGE 17=== (ocr, 2 lines)
   1|                                               ADMINISTRATION
   2|                                                                          16

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|            was a year of progress. Below I would like to highlight the topics that were addressed and the
   2|            accomplishments within the Office of the Town Manager:
   3|            • In July of 2020, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed the Town Manager's
   4|                goals from FY20 and voted to accept the Town Manager's Goals for FY21. The Town Manager's goals
   5|                for FY 2021 included continuing to take the necessary steps towards the redevelopment/reuse of 925
   6|                 Massachusetts Avenue, looking at the feasibility of creating an independent facilities department and
   7|                improve the long term maintenance and repair plan for town facilities, and succession planning. Progress
   8|                 was made on different action items and two of the goals have carried forward into FY 2022.
   9|            • In regards to 925 Massachusetts Avenue, the Town Manager continued to work with the BETA Group on
  10|                 environmental site assessments for that property and with MassDevelopment on any possible funding
  11|                 available to clean-up the property. During FY 2021 the Phase II environmental site assessment was
  12|                 completed and the Town Manager requested additional funding for a Phase III environmental site
  13|                 assessment that would identify the costs of cleanup of the identified hazardous materials and removal of
  14|                 underground tanks. The Town Manager also applied for a Brownfields Redevelopment Fund grant for
  15|                 $250,000 in September of 2020, but unfortunately this property did not yet qualify for that funding. The
  16|                 Town Manager also worked with the DPW Director to hire a surveyor to do survey the property.
  17|            • As it related to one of the action items under the Town Manager's Annual Goals for Facilities, in April the
  18|                 Town Manager announced that a new Facilities Management software called eMaint would be utilized for
  19|                 facility work orders, scheduling preventative maintenance, and overall asset management. This was paid
  20|                 for by a grant through our insurance carrier.
  21|            • The Select Board finalized their Goals for FY 2021 in September of 2020. One of the Select Board goals
  22|                 for FY 2021 was to identify, define and propose at least one project to the 2021 Annual Town Meeting
  23|                 regarding the use and/or re-use of the Old Primary School building, and/or the land on which it sits. The
  24|                 Board held a site visit to the property at the end of October 2020 to review the property and boundaries. In
  25|                 January the Board met with the Architectural Preservation District Commission and the Historical
  26|                 Commission about the Select Board plans to see happen with that property and how to navigate a path
  27|                 forward. The Town Manager and the Select Board worked with Monty Tech on conceptual drawings to
  28|                 demolish the existing old Primary School building and create an a passive recreation/open space area for
  29|                 the Farmers Market, band concerts and other community events. At the May 2021 Annual Town Meeting
  30|                 the Town approved funding to hire a Landscape Architect to prepare conceptual plans for this site and a
  31|                 cost estimate for the overall project.
  32|             • Another one of the Select Board goals for FY 2021 was to review, discuss, and revise (where needed) the
  33|                 re-appointment process of all annual Select Board appointments. In February the Board held a workshop
  34|                 to formalize their process into an appointment policy. Ultimately the Board mostly deferred to the original
  35|                 Annual Select Board Appointment Process but defined the timeline and notifying chairs of the committees
  36|                 of persons wishing to be appointed/reappointed.
  37|                                                                          17

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|            • Also in July the Select Board met with Sean O'Neill of Bright Farms (MA Greenhouse LLC) relative to a
   2|                 proposed major greenhouse that they were looking to build on the land behind Walmart on Massachusetts
   3|                 Avenue and their request for a Tax Increment Financing (TIF) Agreement. The proposal included an
   4|                 initial investment of $10 million and the creation of jobs that would be offered first to Lunenburg
   5|                 residents. After negotiating with Bright Farms over the terms of a TIF agreement, this was voted upon
   6|                 favorably at the November Special Town Meeting. The final TIF provided a 10 year tax discount to the
   7|                 company with a 100% discount in real estate taxes on the new construction in year 1 and decreased by
   8|                 10% each year of the agreement until year 10 where the Town would be receiving 90% of the real estate
   9|                 taxes. In Year 11 Bright Farms would be required to pay the full tax assessment. At the November 2020
  10|                 Special Town Meeting the Town approved the TIF agreement as presented. Ultimately the corporate
  11|                 offices of Bright Farms chose another location that better fit their business model and the project did not
  12|                 move forward.
  13|            • At the beginning of this fiscal year the Town Manager reported that she worked with the Open Space
  14|                 Chairman, the Finance Director, DPW, and Conservation Administrator to ensure that all requirements of
  15|                 the PARC and LAND Grants to purchase the Saliba property were done in order to close on the property
  16|                 in time to submit for reimbursement of these grants. The Town Manager submitted all the required
  17|                 documentation for the LAND and PARC grants, totaling $500,000, to ensure the Town received this
  18|                 reimbursement to purchase this park and open space property.
  19|            • The Town Manager and Select Board conducted an annual review of the Select Board's policies in
  20|                 August and finalized the review in September.
  21|            • This year was another productive year for infrastructure projects in Lunenburg. Besides the continuation
  22|                 of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town began the reconstruction of the Pleasant
  23|                 Street Bridge that was completed in the fall of 2021. As part of paving in the area of Whalom Road, the
  24|                 ADA accessible ramps were incorporated. The Complete Streets design work was finalized during the
  25|                 fiscal year and the construction portion went out to bid in March of 2021. This project wrapped up in the
  26|                 fall of 2021. The Town Manager also worked with the DPW Director on continuing the discussions about
  27|                 the drainage issues on Flat Hill Road and engaging McCarty Engineering to look at drainage solutions.
  28|                 On another section of Flat Hill Road, a culvert project that had received funding in the FY21 Capital Plan
  29|                 had to be delayed due to a revised estimated construction cost for the project due to rising construction
  30|                 and materials cost due to the pandemic that increased the total cost from $428,000 to $750,000. The Town
  31|                 Manager and the DPW Director worked with the engineer to apply for the Small Bridge Grant Program,
  32|                 and seek earmark requests from the State. To date the Town is waiting on the whether the Small Bridge
  33|                 Grant application that was submitted will be approved for funding
  34|             • Due to another building at Tri-Town being built for persons over 55, the Town received a $210,000
  35|                 Density Bonus Payment per MGL c. 40R (Smart Growth Zoning District) from the Department of
  36|                 Housing and Community Development. These funds would close out at the end of FY21 and positively
  37|                 impact the certified free cash available in FY22.
  38|                                                                          18

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                appropriating funds to move forward with the purchase of the streetlights from Unitil in order to
   2|                eventually convert them to LED.
   3|            • After notification from a town resident about a possible dumping site at the Woodruff property (104
   4|                Fairview Road) at the November 2020 Special Town Meeting, the Town Manager engaged a consultant
   5|                that performed an Environmental Site Assessment. In May the Town received the results of that
   6|                assessment that identified the solid waste materials at the site that dated back to the mid-1950's to early
   7|                1970's when it was a farm and prior to the Town purchasing the property. The assessment recommended
   8|                a method of removing and disposing of the solid waste materials and an estimated cost of between
   9|                $72,000 to $84,000. The Town Manager would continue to pursue clean-up at this site and work with the
  10|                Conservation Commission on an Order of Conditions to do the work well into FY22.
  11|            • In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday
  12|                entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses. Throughout the year, the
  13|                Board reviews Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers,, flammable storage licenses, pole
  14|                petitions, tollbooth requests, First Right of Refusal Requests for Chapter 61, properties, and Dangerous
  15|                 Dog hearings.
  16|            • The Town Manager presented a Five Year Forecast and Capital Plan for the funding of 13 projects
  17|                totaling $1,499,825 and a 10 year look ahead at projected capital needs.
  18|            • In February the Select Board met with Town Counsel and the Conservation Commission Chairman to
  19|                 discuss carrying firearms on town property and hunting on conservation land. Due to the recent review of
  20|                the Conservation Commission Land Use Regulations, this topic needed to be addressed due to some areas
  21|                 of inconsistency with the law and the overlap of the authority of the Select Board and Conservation
  22|                 Commission on certain properties.
  23|            •   The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2022 to the Finance Committee and
  24|                 Selectmen in early February. Part of the Town Manager's recommendation was to continue to fund the
  25|                 Other Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the
  26|                 amount of $180,418, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, fund the regular
  27|                 Stabilization fund in the amount of $100,000, and creation of Health Insurance Stabilization Fund to
  28|                 prepare for the FY 2023 increase due to 2 years of significant savings from Holiday Health Insurance
  29|                 Premiums. The FY2022 budget also included the hiring of an additional Full Time Patrolman, an
  30|                 additional Full Time Firefighter, restoration of a facilities technician position, additional hours for the
  31|                 Assistant Town Clerk, a new line for a Board/Committee Clerk expense, a 15% increase for Solid Waste/
  32|                 Recycling Program, and a 2.5% increase for the Lunenburg Public Schools and Monty Tech. The total
  33|                 omnibus budget for FY22 that was approved in May was $39,759,798.
  34|                                                                          19

===PAGE 21=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                electricity prices for the Town's Community Choice Power Supply program and secured a rate with First
   2|                Point Power of $0.11210 for 36 months starting on November 1, 2021.
   3|            •  During fiscal year 2021 there were many projects that the Assistant Town Manager put out a bid
   4|                including the North Cemetery Parking and Accessibility Improvements, the Assessing Services Software,
   5|                a second request to dispose of a piece of 671 West Street, Cleaning Services for Town Buildings,
   6|                Landscaping Services for Town Properties, Moving Services for Library Furniture, Library Re-carpeting,
   7|                Solid Waste and Recycling Services. Other projects that went out to bid in 2021 include the Fuel Tank
   8|                Installation at the DPW, the Pleasant Street Bridge Replacement, the "downtown" Complete Streets
   9|                project, the Entryway Roof Replacement at the Senior Center, the Public Safety Building Carport project,
  10|                and Municipal Trash Bags.
  11|               The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2022. Also in
  12|                June the Board approved a charge to change the Green Communities Task Force to the Green
  13|                Communities Committee and the Board received notification that the owner of Aro Estates, Kevin
  14|                O'Brien, has applied for site eligibility for a 40B project on Howard Street to the State.
  15|            The necessary actions in response to the pandemic continued throughout fiscal year 2021. Changes in
  16|            legislation, continuous changes to capacity limits, COVID policies and procedures, and the rollout of
  17|            vaccines in the latter half of FY 2021 became an integral part of how we needed to approach local
  18|            governance, operations, and public health outreach in vaccination efforts.
  19|            • The limited availability of vaccines due to the number of doses Massachusetts was allocated from the
  20|                 federal government and the high demand was a stress on our community during this time. The Council on
  21|                 Aging assisted seniors with scheduling vaccine appointments and worked with the Nashoba Associated
  22|                Boards of Health on a list of individuals requesting vaccines at vaccination clinics.
  23|            • The towns within the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health requested state approval to create a regional
  24|                vaccination clinic, which was approved by the State in April. The impetus for this was that towns that
  25|                were not in a regional health district were being allotted vaccines by population and this was
  26|                disproportionate to the limited number each of the towns in the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health
  27|                 was receiving.
  28|            • In March of 2021 the American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) was signed into law by the federal government.
  29|                This funding, also called the Coronavirus Local Fiscal Recovery Fund had a distribution of $3.507M for
  30|                Lunenburg. At the inception these funds had very restrictive uses related to the pandemic but over the
  31|                course of many months the US Treasury issued rules that allowed for flexibility on the uses of these
  32|                funds.
  33|            • The State extended certain COVID-19 laws such as outdoor seating for restaurants serving liquor and
  34|                fully remote public meetings.

   1|            Five years has passed since I was appointed your Town Manager and I am thankful for the many residents
   2|            I have met, work with on various committees, and represent on a daily basis overseeing the operations of
   3|            the Town. Similar to last year, the pandemic and neighborly response that is demonstrated by the residents
   4|            of Lunenburg, is inspiring. Thank you to all of our community members who continue to help their fellow
   5|            residents throughout this pandemic.
   6|            I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents
   7|            and business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public. The
   8|            accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without
   9|            the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town
  10|             government turn. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson and Assistant Town Manager
  11|            Julie Belliveau for all your hard work and keeping the office running smoothly. I would also like to thank
  12|            the committee and board members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to
  13|             the residents of town. Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department
  14|             heads that led our various departments in 2021: Thomas Gammel, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire
  15|             Chief; Robert Oliva, DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari,
  16|             Treasurer/Collector; CJ Carroll, Principal Assessor; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA
  17|             Director; Muir Haman, Library Director; Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk; and all their staff for their talent and
  18|             working together to make our town run as efficiently as possible. Lastly, thank you to the residents and
  19|             businesses that make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  20|             Respectfully submitted,
  21|             Heather R. Lemieux

===PAGE 38=== (ocr, 28 lines)
   1|               The Assessors analyze all sales for the prior 18 months to determine new values for the interim adjustments
   2|               in the next fiscal year. Market value has seen increases in the sale of homes in town. Properties have been
   3|               selling over the assessed valuations reflecting an overall increase to market value of the town. The next town
   4|               wide recertification of all properties will be in FY 2024. Home sales have been strong and the town may
   5|               experience some adjustments in valuations for FY 2023.
   6|               Each property in town has a property record card on file with all the components that go into the valuation
   7|               of the parcel such as land size, building description, sales information, etc. We recommend taxpayers
   8|               review their property field card annually to ensure all the information is up to date and correct. The field
   9|               cards are available in our office or online through the Assessor's Data on the Town website.
  10|               The Department of Revenue certified the results of assessment data to be within their statistical
  11|               requirements and the tax bills went out on time. The tax rate went from $18.12 to $17.74 for FY 2021.
  12|               The office continues the process of mandated cyclical inspections of all properties throughout the town.
  13|               This is achieved by inspections, visiting the properties, and by means of sales verification, building permits,
  14|               occupancy permits and abatement inspections. The Board of Assessors strives to maintain accuracy in the
  15|               assessment to sales ratio as well as assessment uniformity.
  16|               The office staff strives to continue to provide good service that the Lunenburg taxpayers have come to
  17|               expect.
  18|               Respectfully submitted,
  19|               Richard Letarte, Chairman         Christine C. Higdon, Member       Kevin Fish, Member

   1|            in the National Register of Historic Places.
   2|            In other business, Certificates to Alter were approved for roof work on several contributing properties. The
   3|            commission advised the Town Manager on appropriate repairs to the Ritter Building and Town Hall. Damage
   4|            to the WWII bronze plaque in Memorial Park was assessed and repairs planned. A Facebook page was
   5|            launched to keep interested parties informed of recent developments. Road signs marking the Architectural
   6|            Preservation District perimeter were replaced and correctly relocated. Finally, the APDC unsuccessfully
   7|            opposed Warrant Article 24 at the spring Annual Town Meeting, which allows the APDC's decisions
   8|            regarding town-owned property to be appealed by vote at Town Meeting.
   9|                                                    BUILDING DEPARTMENT
  10|            The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960
  11|            Massachusetts Avenue. The Building Department consists of the following personnel: Casey Burlingame
  12|            serves as the Building Commissioner as well as the Zoning Officer. Lisa Normandin serves as the
  13|            Administrative Assistant to the Building Commissioner. Jack Biery serves as the Electrical Inspector, and
  14|            Gary Williams serves as the Plumbing and Gas Inspector. The Building Department strives to ensure all work
  15|            done in the Town of Lunenburg is performed to state mandated regulations and codes, as well as local Zoning
  16|            By-Laws. This is to ensure that the buildings built or worked on and all other work done in town has a
  17|            minimum level of state regulated safety. In addition to Building, Plumbing, Gas, and Electrical Permits, the
  18|            Building Department also oversees annual inspections for places of assembly, safety inspections for liquor
  19|            serving establishments, and forms of intent required to issue business certificates from the Town Clerk.
  20|            In fiscal year 2021 the following permits were issued:
  21|            663 Building Permits
  22|            575 Electrical Permits
  23|            160 Plumbing Permits
  24|            172 Gas Permits
  25|            1570 total permits issued
  26|            The state of Massachusetts is currently utilizing the 2015 International Building Code and 2015 International
  27|            Residential Code, as amended by Massachusetts. The Town continues to utilize ViewPoint online permitting
  28|            software, and all applications for work requiring a permit can be done online. In addition, paper applications
  29|            are available at the Building Department office.

   1|            recently updated land use policy. This effort was completed by spring of 2021. The Conservation Commission
   2|            continues to implement revisions to the local wetland regulations, which have been in effect for over two years
   3|            to date and have severed the interests of protecting valuable community natural resources very fairly and
   4|            effectively.
   5|            The Commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to
   6|            meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The
   7|            Commission continues to work with the Town on familiarizing itself with the continually changing storm
   8|            water mandates from the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary
   9|            codification as well as aiding with the community implementations the "MS4" permit regulations.
  10|            The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans using grants as outlined in the
  11|            last two annual reports. The upcoming management efforts will be concentrated on the RAYNO property on
  12|            Northfield Road and the Hawes property on Hollis Road. The stewardship plan for the Hollis Road property is
  13|            oriented toward enhancing bird wildlife habitat in conjunction with the Mass Audubon Society as part of the
  14|            DCR "Forestry for the Birds"      program. The forest stewardship plans provide environmental management
  15|            plans which include timber stand management. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup of New England
  16|            Forestry Consultants who is a noted forester with years of experience working in Lunenburg and the adjacent
  17|            towns/cities serving municipalities and private landowners. The Commission continues to hold public forums
  18|            on the implementation of its open space policies and timber-stand management. The Commission expects to
  19|            continue to apply for and receive funding from the Commonwealth of MA to author stewardship plans for our
  20|             Conservation Areas.
  21|            The Commission was awarded and is implementing a Recreational Trails Program grant providing color coded
  22|            trail markers and coordinated "You Are Here" trail map signs to expand the safe use of Lunenburg's existing
  23|            trail network. Regional trail system map signs would show how trails are interconnected among multiple
  24|            properties. The project will also provide informational kiosks and emergency access gates. During 2021, trail
  25|            maps were developed for, and continue to be refined in detail, for eleven conservation properties. These trail ion properties. These trail
  26|             maps are posted on the new Conservation Commission Trails website, on informational kiosks on the
  27|             properties, and on "You Are Here" trail map signs at trail heads and trail intersections. The Commission
  28|            would like to express our gratitude for all of the hard working volunteers that work so enthusiastically in
  29|            executing the project.
  30|             The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley Improvement Corporation and Hickory
  31|             Hills Landowners Inc. on the implementations of their lake management plans. These plans set goals and
  32|            action items designed to give a better picture of the overall health of the lakes and define various strategies to
  33|             ensure continued protection of these valuable resources.
  34|             The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!

   1|            The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Select Board. The
   2|            FY21 Council on Aging Board members were: Chairperson Deb Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson Jane Rabbitt, Barbara
   3|            Brown, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Pete Lincoln, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John Pesenti, Susan O'Sullivan, and
   4|            Judith Tarbell.
   5|            The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Supporters
   6|            Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett
   7|            Regional Transit Authority.
   8|            We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY21 we had 36 volunteers who contributed
   9|            883.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on Wheels program, program
  10|            leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, and friendly visitors/phone calls. We greatly appreciate their
  11|            service.
  12|            Although the pandemic has made it necessary to change the day to day operations at the senior center, we were still able
  13|            to offer a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, transportation, health and wellness, arts and crafts,
  14|            intergenerational, bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, Memory Café, health
  15|            insurance counseling, and social events both at the senior center and offsite to people age 50+.
  16|            There were approximately 773 people who utilized the senior center in FY21. Those 773 people compiled 17,748 units
  17|            of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  18|            The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
  19|            www.mycommunityonline.com, or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall,
  20|            Library, Post Office, local churches, and various businesses throughout the community.
  21|            We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  22|            supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
  23|            Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provided funding for new
  24|            living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep at the Eagle House
  25|            Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for their time and funding for support of
  26|            programming for our clients.
  27|            In closing, I want to recognize the staff and volunteers at the Eagle House Senior Community Center for their
  28|            unwavering commitment to our Lunenburg elders. Our staff and volunteers are to be commended on their dedication,
  29|            flexibility, and strength that they have shown throughout these challenging times, and I thank you all.
  30|            Respectfully submitted
  31|            Susan Doherty, Director
  32|            Lunenburg Council on Aging

   1|                Town s residents and visitors, and to provide a cost ejective and environmentally souna means of
   2|                disposing of solid waste and wastewater.
   3|            DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY
   4|            Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything worthwhile can be
   5|            accomplished.
   6|            Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but will not require
   7|            repeated attempts.
   8|            Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
   9|            Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by the men and
  10|            women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a seamless flow of
  11|            administrative support.
  12|            DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  13|            Public Works staff is either working or on call 24 hours a day 365 days a year prepared to respond to any
  14|            weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise.
  15|            We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure others are
  16|            aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  17|            dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of service.
  18|            Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have highlighted the fact that
  19|            Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency management as first responders.
  20|            Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required
  21|            services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  22|            This department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of the fact that the
  23|            common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals,
  24|            service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling
  25|            operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal,
  26|            cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting everyone in the community. We will continue to
  27|            engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual budget requests, capital
  28|            requests, and specific projects.
  29|            DEPARTMENTAL GOALS FOR FISCAL 2022
  30|            • Continue to coordinate administrative directives with the activities of all public service departments and
  31|                organizations within the DPW to improve service levels. Strive always for a higher level of efficiency.
  32|            • Coordinate with those who work daily in Town facilities to best prioritize capital improvements. DPW
  33|                 plays a major role in the enhancement of the work environment of all Town employees.
  34|            • Increase involvement with our insurance provider to make better use of available resources in an effort to
  35|                 obtain credits toward lessening premiums. Safety is our first concern.
  36|            • Encourage and facilitate DPW personnel to obtain and maintain required licensure in order to provide an
  37|                 optimal level of service.

===PAGE 59=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|             It had become a high priority goal of the Select Board as Road Commissioners to achieve a pavement
   2|             condition of 72%-75% town-wide. While State supported Chapter 90 funds remain stagnant, the dedicated
   3|             Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially. Additionally, with the
   4|             infusion of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported by residents, the
   5|             Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing smooth and safe
   6|             transportation for our residents and all those travelling through our community.
   7|             The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the total paving
   8|             value of years past as we attack a nine-million-dollar backlog of repairs.
   9|             Time spent addressing pot holes and other roadway deficiencies diminish substantially as new paving
  10|             increases. A clear benefit of the dollars expended. There still remains work to do, but expectations are
  11|             high.
  12|             Funding for the reconstruction of the Pleasant Street Bridge was obtained through the State's Small Bridge
  13|             Program. Working with our engineering consultant VHB and contractor R. Bates & Son, construction
  14|             began in May 2021 with an anticipated completion date in August 2021. Work also continues on the
  15|             Complete Streets Design to enhance walkability in the Town Center, with the expectation of the
  16|             construction work being put out to bid in spring of 2021.
  17|             Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be viable due to
  18|             the extraordinary expense.
  19|             FLEET MAINTENANCE
  20|             The DPW Maintenance Division is responsible for the repair and preventative maintenance to all town
  21|             vehicles and equipment and is carried out by a single, skilled and dedicated fleet mechanic with support by
  22|             department personnel. High performance police units, first responder fire and rescue apparatus, park,
  23|             cemetery and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by the
  24|             Maintenance Division. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore
  25|             coote cuss ty. Dea to the volatie global refore there to sis of cland petroleum based products
  26|             Use of a fleet maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool. Each repair is logged by
  27|             department and vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database. Every vehicle can be
  28|             referenced for part numbers, required services, as well and repair costs over the life of the equipment.
  29|             Preventative maintenance continues to be a most valuable component of the program. Work will continue
  30|             to update and evaluate a vehicle replacement schedule based upon American Public Works Association
  31|             recommendations in order to more clearly represent the capital needs of the department.

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|            with a stable of reliable contractors, the department delivers services directly or oversees those functions
   2|            provided by specialized contractors.
   3|            Moving forward the five year Capital Plan for facilities in 2022, as presented by the department will focus on
   4|            the needs of the public building envelopes utilizing the reports prepared by Vertex as a guide. Protecting and
   5|            maintaining the Town building exteriors is a priority even with the uncertain futures of some of the buildings.
   6|            This department remains keenly interested in the proposed T.C. Passios building reuse plan and how it
   7|            impacts the future use of Town Hall and the Ritter Building. A clearly defined plan for the future use of all
   8|            buildings would facilitate planning of capital expenditures.
   9|            ENVIRONMENTAL
  10|            Storm Water
  11|            DPW works to meet the obligations of the permit and required reporting each year.
  12|            The DPW provides maintenance and care of the town's storm water management system. This includes but is
  13|            not limited to: catch basing cleaning, street sweeping, pipe jetting, pipe replacement and swale cleaning. The
  14|            department responds to calls for clogged drains and pipes to help control ponding and flooding.
  15|            Wastewater
  16|            Working with the Sewer Commission, the DW continues to be responsible for the day to day operation and
  17|            maintenance of the wastewater collection system. Maintenance of the collection system inclusive of 10 pump
  18|            stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but continues to be the responsibility of the DPW. Business
  19|            management, inspections, and permitting remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
  20|            Tree Removal
  21|            Storm damage together with natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning that typically
  22|            exceeds the department's allotted budget for tree removal. The department strives to fulfill each resident
  23|            request but must prioritize work based upon the perceived degree of danger to residents and property. With
  24|            the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established
  25|            through prioritization. Currently, the amount of work far exceeds the funding available for it. As this is the
  26|            case, the DPW will continue to act in a reactionary manner as needs arise. All work aside from brush
  27|            removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the necessity
  28|            of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff works to remove and dispose
  29|            of woody debris within the town's right-of-way along with a town-wide program of selective curbside
  30|            mowing and vegetation clearing. An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the burden of
  31|            the Town by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  32|            Landfill
  33|            The capped, closed landfill off Young's Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
  34|            by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as
  35|            well as groundwater contamination.
  36|            The frequency of the testing reduced through a special permit, as a result of consistently favorable results,
  37|            remained constant throughout the year.

   2|              Solid Waste
   3|              Solid waste is collected under the Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program by our new vendor EJ Harvey who
   4|              replaced Casella in 2021 via a public bid process. An Invitation for Bids was solicited and Harvey was
   5|              the only bidder for the contract, and after review by the Town was awarded the contract. Under this
   6|              contract recycling is also collected by EJ Harvey. Our PAYT bag supplier continues to coordinate the
   7|              effort between supplier and vendor. DPW remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor
   8|              responsible for curbside pick-up.
   9|              Lake Shirley Dam
  10|              In the early 1990's with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, the Lake Shirley Association turned
  11|              over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
  12|              maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the
  13|              Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  14|               establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella
  15|              of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA/DEP mandates,
  16|              compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a
  17|               professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a
  18|               positive report and we remain in total compliance.
  19|              Winter Operations
  20|              Snow and ice present a major challenge. The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DW to
  21|              maintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icing
  22|              events. To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with
  23|               flexibility to accommodate the unpredictability of each storm. The expectation of immediate service is
  24|              high and must be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  25|               Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the Capital Plan. Financial support
  26|              is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation ensuring minimal interruption of service due to breakdowns
  27|              and repairs. In the fall salt is delivered to the DPW facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other
  28|              communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price. From late summer to early fall vehicle
  29|              maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, spreaders, and plows for winter operations. During the
  30|               construction season road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects
  31|              in order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter
  32|               operations. Typically, DPW staff combines with subcontractors to provide comprehensive snow and ice
  33|              operations.
  34|               Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost. For
  35|               example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing
  36|              rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically and as allowed by State law, the
  37|               department's snow removal accounts operate in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost
  38|               of snow removal operations for an average winter.
  39|               The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private
  40|               contractors who tirelessly and successfully challenge the worst nature has to offer regardless of severity or
  41|               duration.
  42|                                                                          60

   1|             the Town Beach. The Town Beach is staffed with employees of the Parks and Recreation Department during

   5|             CEMETERY
   6|             One dedicated Cemetery Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South

===PAGE 63=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|             Annual Town meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on warrant
   2|            articles that directly impact how we manage and resource our town. The town budget articles are core
   3|             elements and the capstone of a months-long process. Throughout the year the Town Manager and
   4|             Department Heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful conversations about how to best serve the
   5|            town. While developing the budget they consider funding, contracts, and mandates, as well as what the
   6|             community wants and needs to thrive. The Town Manager finalized and presented her proposed FY2022
   7|             budget to the Select Board and Finance Committee in early February. Department Heads each presented
   8|            their budgets over several Finance Committee meetings, this year all done safely through the magic of Zoom
   9|             video teleconference calls. Concurrently the Superintendent of Schools and School Committee developed the
  10|             Lunenburg School District budget, culminating in a public hearing and approval vote on March 17". The
  11|             Finance Committee conducted a public hearing and voted its recommendation on the enclosed budget on
  12|             March 25th
  13|             While the FY2022 budget was developed in anticipation of a post-pandemic environment, the effects of the
  14|             pandemic are likely to reverberate with respect to revenues, available funds, and expenditures through the
  15|             coming fiscal year. Fortunately, Lunenburg has fared reasonably-well financially thus far. Our projections
  16|             and visibility are clearer than they were at both the Annual and Special Town Meetings during Calendar
  17|             Year 2020. The Finance Committee is optimistic of the town's ability to meet the prioritized needs of each
  18|             department throughout the coming year, while still taking a conservative stance on revenue projections,
  19|             funding for reserve accounts, and heavily scrutinizing all spending increases.
  20|             Factors affecting this outlook include:
  21|             COVID-19 related Grant Funding:
  22|            • $753,293 in remaining available Cares Act funds, expiring in December 2021
  23|            • $588,834 in available ESSER II funding, expiring in September 2023
  24|            • Unknown new/additional funding from state and federal legislation such as the American Rescue Plan
  25|                 Act that was approved in March.
  26|             Revenue:
  27|            • Relatively flat local aid funding, 0.17% increase in the governor's budget
  28|            • Local receipts have held up well, exceeding FY20 collections by 15.75% thus far
  29|             One time savings:
  30|            • 75% of Monty Tech's ESSER II funds have been applied to assessments, $40,758 for
  31|                 Lunenburg
  32|            • Health Insurance Savings of $369,334, including a full month "premium holiday"
  33|             This year's budget process has evolved into an exercise in cautious appropriation. Substantial funds available
  34|             this year, likely won't be in the next. How can we best meet the demonstrated needs of our departments
  35|             while ensuring stability in the future?
  36|                                                                          62

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|            and sustaining COVID19-related grant funding, this year's 2.5% budget increase meets the Superintendent's
   2|            recommended needs assessment. While this is good news, it is tempered by the short- term nature of the
   3|            above-mentioned health insurance savings and grant funding. Further, state aid remains relatively flat, a
   4|            consequence in-part of the Student Opportunity Act which shifted priorities to lower-income communities.
   5|            Without restoration of meaningful increases in Chapter 70 funding, the school budget will be constrained by
   6|            the tax levy in future years.
   7|            • The target budgets for the Police and Fire Departments were an exception to budgets that reflected
   8|                maintaining existing staffing levels. In 2018 the Police and Fire Chiefs developed long term staffing
   9|                plans. These plans were created to address the staffing needs of these public safety departments that over
  10|                the years have not kept up with the growth of the town and translated to additional service calls for our
  11|                public safety departments. Since 2018 we have adhered to the Five Year Staffing Plan for the Fire
  12|                Department that adds one additional Full Time Career Firefighter each year until FY2023 and the Ten
  13|                 Year Staffing Plan for the Police Department that adds one additional Full Time Patrol Officer each year
  14|                until FY2028. The FY2022 preliminary budget proposes an additional Patrol Officer and an additional
  15|                 Career Firefighter to continue to follow these staffing plans. The Finance Committee also noted
  16|                anticipated costs in future years due to unfunded mandates related to the police reform act.
  17|            • The FY22 Capital Plan will be funded with free cash, unexpended capital, and tax revenues. The
  18|                 FY22 Capital Plan recommendation at this time is to fund 15 out of the 19 prioritized capital
  19|                project requests totaling $1,597,825. The Town Manager's budget recommends transferring
  20|                $250,000 from free cash into the special purpose stabilization account, which was created to fund
  21|                 large vehicle and equipment purchases and lessen the dependence on tax revenues in future years.
  22|            • Revenues from property taxes includes a conservative estimate for New Growth, $280,590,
  23|                 which is consistent with trends of the past few years.
  24|            • This year's DPW budget includes an additional facilities staff member and part-time, seasonal
  25|                 help for cemetery maintenance and upkeep. This budget also resumes the practice of annual
  26|                 $50,000 increases for pavement management.
  27|            • Some of our departments fund regular, ongoing expenses with donations and grants. Those
  28|                 funding sources are not guaranteed but the services they fund are vital.
  29|            Looming decisions about town buildings have also captured the attention of the Finance Committee
  30|            during this budget season. Lunenburg's debt ratio has been repeatedly noted by financial advisors
  31|            as a negative factor in our otherwise very good credit rating. There will be a significant reduction
  32|            in debt service as we pay off the Public Safety Building and Primary School loans in 2024.
  33|             However, it is likely we will need to take on more debt to cover future needs. Currently the town is
  34|            analyzing a T.C. Passios building restoration project, which is considered in articles in this warrant.
  35|             We will likely need to renovate or replace Turkey Hill Elementary School in the late 2020's or
  36|            early 2030's. There are no easy answers for debt management.

   1|            • Worked with the Cemetery Commission on the plan for the restoration of the 19th century

   4|                Arnold/Litch cemetery.

   6|                stone directional markers.

   9|                homes under the title "Historically Speaking".
  10|            • Work was done on the town survey of historic homes, particularly the "K" houses documented on the
  11|                1833 Cyrus Kilburn Map.
  12|            • The Town's 19th century standing stone directional markers and other town monuments received their
  13|                 annual inspection.
  14|            • Research was started on slavery in Lunenburg.
  15|            • Successfully proposed amendments to strengthen and specify reporting requirements of the town's
  16|                 demolition delay bylaw.
  17|             Since the turn of the century, Lunenburg has lost historic houses and buildings at the average rate of 1 per
  18|             year. Among the houses and buildings lost were eight (8) that were on the historic 1833 Cyrus Kilburn map.
  19|            With the town owned historic Brooks House and the 1925 Lunenburg High School on the road to demolition,
  20|            at the present rate of demolition, few historic homes and buildings will be left in Lunenburg in 100 years.
  21|            Strong advocacy by the Select Board and town government, as well as strong public support and involvement,
  22|            would be needed to reverse this trend. The Commission has gained one (1) new member, Megan Lordan. We
  23|            welcome her and her talents and we are sure that she will be a valuable contributor to the Commission's work.
  24|            The Commission presently has five (5) members.

   1|             Fiscal year 2021 was filled with disruption, but our library staff focused on improving the value of the library
   2|             for the residents of Lunenburg. The fiscal year opened with all town facilities closed to the public and it
   3|              closed soon after we launched one of our most successful summer reading programs ever with an event
   4|             attended by more than 250 people.
   5|             Despite so much uncertainty and tumult over the course of the year, the library expanded its resources and
   6|             worked to finish the first strategic plan for the library in more than 10 years. Our plan ensures that we will be
   7|             eligible for federal and state grant funding, and we plan to apply for this funding for the FY23 budget cycle.
   8|             As part of that planning process, the library's mission statement was also revised to more accurately and
   9|             concisely describe our current efforts:
  10|                      Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community, enriching
  11|                      our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.
  12|             The plan itself is a document that shows the library's current state and will guide our efforts over the next five
  13|             years. The plan reflects the high aspirations of the trustees and the staff of the library, and it was only possible
  14|             with the participation of dozens of community members and hours of effort. The plan is available on the
  15|             library's website: lunenburglibrary.org
  16|             We have seen a continuation of the trends that started in FY2020, with increased usage of our digital
  17|             resources. We have delivered additional selection in that realm in anticipation and in response. We purchased
  18|             more e-books and e-audiobooks to meet demand, and we added resources like Kanopy, a streaming video
  19|             service which allows you to view popular and enriching videos from home.
  20|             Library staff launched a second Instagram account for teens and our digital presence continues to grow. As a
  21|             result, we have seen different and new faces at the library at programs like our Adult Craft Lab and to take
  22|             home one of our craft kits or STEM learning kits. Although the total number of visits was limited by a
  23|             number of factors in FY2021, the books, DVDs, magazines, programs, and people that are included in the
  24|             library resources are still available.
  25|             We partnered again with Hannaford, this time to bring a cooking series to children and families with the on-
  26|             staff nutritionist in Lunenburg. Many of our programs this year were also held in the digital realm for the first
  27|             time. Zoom and Facebook Live enabled us to offer virtual author visits, lectures, art classes and lots more
  28|             even though people were not meeting in person. We utilized the opportunity to partner with other area
  29|             libraries to bring cultural enrichment that we might not otherwise have been able to provide.
  30|             One of the biggest projects of the year was the installation of new carpet throughout the building. Thanks to
  31|             capital plan funding from the town, all of our aging carpet was replaced. To enable this work, a company
  32|             specializing in moving libraries was employed to moved our collections and furniture. The work was
  33|             impressive and the results maintain the impressive appearance of our incredible facility. Most first-time
  34|             visitors to the library are surprised that the building is now more than 15 years old.

   7|              Summary
   8|              FY21 was a "catch up" year for PAC. Programming and services were maintained, while strategic plans
   9|              were on hold and improvements slow. Due to the pandemic in FY20 PAC began moving to normalcy in
  10|              FY21 increasing both services and local programming. PAC offered more videos of government meetings
  11|              on the LunenburgAccess YouTube channel and live Facebook feeds than ever before due to Zoom recorded
  12|              meetings and broadcasts, as we began to broaden use of additional technology.
  13|              PAC had to keep up with an everchanging environment in FY21 by creating a Zoom Hybrid meeting room
  14|              at Town Hall, at the request of the Town Manager. Broadcasting required a new set of procedures and
  15|              training of videographers. Now, the community can attend a meeting in person, or through Zoom providing
  16|              improved flexibility in meeting participation.
  17|              Moving forward, PAC hopes to continue to grow through the addition of videographers, new programs and
  18|              outreach in FY22. The educational channel has begun to add programs including events and sports at
  19|              schools, with PAC videographers on location. In addition to school events, the studio produced two
  20|              programs that were added to the MassAccess database in FY21 proven to be wildly popular around the state.
  21|               A current goal is to reach out to the community and Town board members to use public access services.
  22|              With additional staff and videographers, PAC will be positioned to make FY22 a memorable year.
  23|              Won't you join us as a producer or volunteer? PAC's supportive staff offers production
  24|              assistance, making it easy to produce your own show. Our community is thriving with talented and creative
  25|              individuals - now is your time to shine! If producing doesn't appeal to you, then consider "behind the
  26|              camera" options. Become a volunteer. Volunteers are the "life blood" of media centers around the state, and
  27|              without that involvement PAC will not be able to grow. Consider volunteering to help provide the
  28|              necessary resources needed for PAC's continued success. WE NEED YOU!
  29|                                                                          73

   1|            have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants work in various town departments doing jobs
   2|            such as general office work, custodial, library shelving, Meals on Wheels, kitchen assistance duties, etc., to
   3|             earn the tax abatement.
   4|            The 2021 tax abatement maximum is $1,500 and applied to your 3'd quarter real estate tax bill.
   5|            Eligibility Requirements
   6|             • Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
   7|            • Must occupy and be the owner of the property as of July 1st
   8|            • Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name (60 years+)
   9|            • Meet the income guidelines of (proof of income is required):
  10|                    One person household - $50,000
  11|                    Two person household - $75,000
  12|             • Earnings are exempt from state taxes
  13|            • Earnings are subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester County
  14|                 Regional Retirement or Massachusetts Teachers' Retirement System
  15|            • Final amount earned will be applied to the 3d quarter real estate tax
  16|            • All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check
  17|            • All Property Tax Workers must reapply and be accepted annually for participation in the program

   1|             2006. As of 7/1/20, the Commission consisted of Dave MacDonald-Chair, John Reynolds-Vice Chair,
   2|             Brett Ramsden-Clerk and Members Mike Deveikis and Carl Luck. Due to Covid-19 restrictions, all
   3|             meetings were held virtually via Zoom, with the exception of the 6/29/21 meeting that was held in person
   4|             at the Lunenburg Town Hall.
   5|             At the 2/23/21 meeting, Carl Luck announced he would be resigning from the Commission, effective
   6|             4/30/21, after serving for 15 years. Upon his departure with 1 year left of his term, he encouraged a sewer
   7|             customer to run for his remaining term. The Commission thanked Carl for his many years of service and
   8|             expertise to the Commission. Carl was involved from the beginning in 2006, working to develop the
   9|             Comprehensive Wastewater Management Plan, the sewer district by-law and Sewer Service Area. He
  10|             worked on negotiating the Fitchburg IMA and Highfield Village Allocation. The Commission wished
  11|             Carl the best and hoped he would enjoy his retirement.
  12|              The Town Caucus was held in person on 3/15/21 and the nominees were Dave MacDonald -3- year term
  13|             and Mike Nault -1 year term. The Town Election was held on 5/15/21 and both candidates won their
  14|             respective terms. At the May 25, 2020 meeting, the Commission re-elected Dave MacDonald-Chair, John
  15|              Reynolds-Vice Chair and Brett Ramsden-Clerk, along with Members Mike Deveikis and Mike Nault.
  16|             FY21 saw the Commission bid farewell to long time DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz, who retired
  17|              10/31/20 after 30 years of service to the town. The Commissioners thanked Jack for all his support and
  18|              work to ensure the smooth running of the sewer system.
  19|             In November 2020, the Commission welcomed Rob Oliva as the new DPW Director and looked forward
  20|             to working with him to insure a smooth transition. The Commission started working with Rob on
  21|             implementing the Maintenance Plan developed by Wright Pierce in FY20. During the budget setting
  22|              process for FY22, the Commission voted to move $100K from Retained Earnings into Capital
  23|              Expenditures to fund necessary repairs and replacement of equipment, 1/I (Inflow/Infiltration) repairs and
  24|              other big-ticket items needed for the smooth running of the pump stations. Specific projects were
  25|              replacement of a pump at the Meadow Woods station and installation of a new control panel at the
  26|              Electric Ave. station. In addition, Green Mountain was hired during FY21 to clean, inspect and do grout
  27|             sealing of the joints on the Elizabeth Street main, to address long term I/I issue brought to light by the
  28|              Weston & Sampson 1/1 report.
  29|              At the beginning of FY21, the Commission developed an RFP for the Operation & Maintenance of the
  30|              sewer stations and put it out for bid. CTE (Chad Empey), who was currently providing excellent service on the stations on a time & materials basis won the bid to continue with a new contract, beginning on
  31|              9/26/20 and renewing at the end of June 2021. CTE also set up a Generator Maintenance program with
  32|              Weld, for the upkeep on the pump station generators.
  33|              The Commission also approved a contract with Flow Assessment for the monitoring and upkeep of the
  34|              flow meters to Fitchburg and Leominster, In addition, the contract included the lease of equipment, which
  35|              will be updated, maintained and replaced as needed by Flow Assessment. The accuracy of the meters is
  36|              crucial since Lunenburg is now billed according to the flows going through those meters.
  37|              There are currently 909 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 330 which have access
  38|              to town sewer, but are not yet connected. During FY21, 20 new accounts connected to town sewer. The
  39|              Commission billed out $999,817 in quarterly sewer bills and had $37,435 of prior year delinquencies
  40|              added to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment in December 2020.
  41|                                                                          75

===PAGE 77=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|             more accurate billing.
   2|             The Commission was notified in January 2021 that Fitchburg Wastewater had updated their Sewer Use
   3|             Ordinance (Chapter 147) which is incorporated into the Lunenburg Sewer Use Regulations as required by
   4|             the Inter-Municipal Agreement with Fitchburg. The Commissioners reviewed the requested policy changes,
   5|             updating the Regulations and had Town Counsel review and comment. The updates were sent back to
   6|             Fitchburg for their review and comment. Once the changes are approved and finalized, 2 public hearings will
   7|             be set up in FY22 for approving the Regulation updates and the Commission will also look at changes to
   8|             other policies that have been discussed throughout the year.
   9|             In FY21, the Commission continued the annual permit program for the 19 Food Service Establishments with
  10|              grease traps and interceptors on Town Sewer. The Commission voted to waive the Annual Permit fee for the
  11|              Grease Trap Program, as many restaurants were still struggling to remain open due to COVID-19
  12|             restrictions. The "Rules & Regulations for Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments"
  13|             continues to be a focus of the Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on
  14|             commercial and public disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease
  15|             into the system which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down
  16|             the drain.
  17|             It had been recommended by the Town Accountant that the transfers to Stabilization Funds of any monies
  18|             collected and not expended throughout a fiscal year for specific purposes would be transferred at the Special
  19|              Town Meeting following the close of the prior fiscal year. The two funds affected by this change are the
  20|              Sewer Bank-Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund and the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund. A portion of
  21|             the connection charges assessed at the time of an application for a permit to connect go into these accounts
  22|             and target resolving I/l issues and future capacity costs paid to the cities of Fitchburg and Leominster for
  23|             increased capacities. Therefore, at the Special Town Meeting in November 2020, the amount of $68,097.59
  24|             was transferred to the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund.
  25|              At the 5/1/21 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward three articles, all of which were related to the
  26|              FY22 budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY22, $831,406.99 was
  27|             appropriated from Sewer Enterprise revenues, $40,765 was transferred from the Stabilization Funds, and the
  28|             sum of $301,113.71 was appropriated from Retained Earnings for a total appropriation of $1,173,486.10 to
  29|              operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY22. The amount of $780,279.05 was also transferred to the General
  30|             Fund to cover the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service on the sewer betterments. The
  31|             second article transferred monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $30,929.43,
  32|             approximately 3% of proposed revenues as the Commission had done for FY21. This fund is to be used to
  33|             maintain and improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. The
  34|             third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles
  35|              passed at Town Meeting.
  36|              During FY21, the Commission met with several groups, looking to connect to Town sewer. Bright Farms, a
  37|             lettuce greenhouse business, met several times with the Commission throughout FY21, but withdrew the
  38|              project in early FY22. A potential 40B project for Howard Street and a development at 5 Chestnut St. were
  39|              also discussed. The Commission always welcomes interest in new sewer connections and is always willing
  40|             to work with property owners, developers and engineers to discuss expansion of the sewer system.
