   1|               discretionary spending.
   2|              Veterans, families or Widows who qualify can be provided monthly financial assistance (reimbursement) for
   3|               specific living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care and pharmaceutical co-payments. With the VSO's
   4|               approval, the Town of West Boylston provides the financial assistance, but then the Town is reimbursed by the
   5|               State's Department of Veterans Services (DVS). It is the function of the Veterans' Service Office to identify
   6|               qualifying members of the community and assist them with the enrollment into the DVS Program. Additionally,
   7|               çertain Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from the Veterans Administration (VA).
   8|               Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.
   9|              During Fiscal Year 2021 (July 2020 - June 2021), Lunenburg served an average of 10 clients (Veterans, Families or
  10|              Widows) per month. Lunenburg paid out $71,999.46 in benefits, or an average of $620.77 per client per month.
  11|              Lunenburg was reimbursed $53,999.60 by the DVS which is 75% of what was given as assistance; the maximum
  12|              allowable share.
  13|              Some examples of Veterans Service Office assistance services include:
  14|              • Enrollment in Chapter 115 State Veterans benefits
  15|              • Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations
  16|              • Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges
  17|              • Housing assistance, including Veteran Shelters and Veterans Homes
  18|              • Payments of "Welcome Home" bonuses for deployed veterans
  19|              • Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates
  20|              • Employment Assistance
  21|              The Veterans' Service Office also provides information, advice and assistance to any citizen interested in learning of
  22|               their qualifications for federal benefits, such as:
  23|              • VA Disability payments and property tax exemptions
  24|              • Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those Killed in Action, or for those who are declared to be
  25|                  100% disabled from service-connected conditions
  26|              • VA Pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance and death benefits
  27|              • VA Medical Care for both physical and psychological needs
  28|              • Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill)
  29|              • VA Home Loans
  30|              • Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veteran or civilian cemeteries)

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|             The COVID-19 Pandemic has continued to affect our community and department, though we have been
   2|             fortunate that we have had minimal impacts to our staff. Regardless of the Pandemic's impact, our personnel
   3|             do not have the option of "working remote". They continue to be ready to respond no matter the conditions.
   4|             My thanks go out to the men and women of the department who continue to persevere though this strange
   5|             time in our history.
   6|                     Current Fire Department staffing is now eight Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and Emergency
   7|             Medical Technicians, plus one full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The Station is manned
   8|             with 2-3 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians 24 hours a day, 7 days/week. This coverage is a
   9|             combination of Career personnel in conjunction with 96 hours per week of per diem coverage. This coverage
  10|             is provided by our Call Firefighter/EMT's staffing the Station at their hourly rate.          Staffing the station with
  11|             dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs.
  12|                     Though we have 24-hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire calls still require
  13|             more personnel than are on duty in the station. This means we still rely heavily on the dedication and
  14|             commitment of our On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. Our Call personnel remain
  15|             and will remain a vital part of our emergency response for years to come.            A new Call Firefighter/EMT must
  16|             attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training. Members
  17|             qualifying to drive Fire Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training.
  18|             On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two years to full complete this training.
  19|                      The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level
  20|             Ambulance Service. Firefighters Austin Flagg and Carl Gamberdella have obtained their Paramedic
  21|             certifications and two more members are enrolled in Paramedic training. Paramedic training is in excess of
  22|             2000 hours and requires anywhere from 9-18 months of college level classroom education, clinical time and
  23|             field internships to obtain certification. The department continues to train our newly hired staff to this level
  24|             and recruit licensed Paramedics to fill Career positions when possible. Our goal is to begin providing
  25|             Paramedic level service to the community sometime in 2022.
  26|             In 2021, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 521 inspections; this is in addition to multiple fire drills and
  27|             public education initiatives. By law, we are required to inspect smoke and carbon monoxide detector
  28|             installations when a home is sold. We are also required to inspect all new oil burner and propane gas
  29|             installations. Any business holding an on-site liquor license also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety
  30|             issues. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as
  31|             well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during
  32|             2021 include:
  33|             Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety
  34|             Education in both the schools and with senior citizens. This grant provides funding for both materials and
  35|             personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Highlights of activity through
  36|             this grant include:
  37|             • Though the Pandemic still affects our interactions with the schools and the elderly, we continue to
  38|                 provide services where and when we can. Both fire safety education for primary school age children and
  39|                 the "Sand for Seniors" Program have been reinstituted.
  40|             • Personnel assisted with several community events, such as the Lion's Club Thanksgiving Basket
  41|                                                                         86

===PAGE 88=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|             homes for smoke and carbon monoxide detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to
   2|             install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant. Despite the Pandemic, we modified our
   3|             procedures to keep this valuable program operating.
   4|             It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke
   5|             detectors.
   6|             Seven Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive
   7|              Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and unusual rescue situations, such as Confined Space,
   8|             Rescue from Heights,         Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and
   9|             Recovery. In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/
  10|              Trench Rescue Trailer.
  11|              Apparatus replacement has reached a plateau for a few years, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all
  12|             under 10 years old. The oldest pumper, Squad 3 is approaching 14 years old. In 2021, this unit underwent a
  13|             mid-life overhaul intended to extend its service life. The overhaul included mechanical work, upgrades to its
  14|             warning devices and paint/body repairs. The is unit doubles as our Heavy Rescue unit and responds to motor
  15|              vehicle accidents and special rescue situations as well as firefighting duties.
  16|             to protect the wearers from cancer causing materials that are often byproducts of today's synthetic materials
  17|              burning. The cost of the equipment, like many other things has steadily risen over several years as well.
  18|              This funding will put our personnel in a good set up equipment with a slightly used backup set. This will
  19|              allow all of our personnel to remain "in service" if their primary gear has to be cleaned.
  20|                      Grant funding is also allowing us to replace our 20+ year old forest fire protective clothing. This
  21|              equipment is a lighter weight version of the heavier, bulkier structural firefighting gear. State grant funding
  22|              will replace this equipment with a dual-purpose type of clothing that it suitable for both brush fires and motor
  23|              vehicle/technical rescue situations. An added benefit is this will reduce the wear and tear on the Structural
  24|              firefighting clothing.
  25|                      In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  26|              me every day. Especially in the face of the ongoing Pandemic. I would also like to thank the other i would also tike to thank the ther
  27|              department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help. The level of interdepartmental
  28|              cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business in invisible to many people, but it is critical to
  29|              all operations. Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing support of
  30|              public safety. Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to
  31|              expect from us.
  32|              Patrick A. Sullivan

   1|             Mission Statement:
   2|                We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police
   3|               services to our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the
   4|               benefits that are realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have
   5|               been developed with our community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will
   6|               strive to deliver police services in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while
   7|               respecting all individual rights. We will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes,
   8|               apprehend criminal offenders, and help resolve quality of life issues.
   9|              Values:
  10|             • We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.
  11|             • We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the
  12|                 community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department.
  13|             • We are a proactive and problem solving agency.

   3|              continued to deal with the effects of COVID-19. We were also tasked with navigating the challenges that
   4|              we have been mandated in accordance with the passing of "An Act relative to justice, equity, and
   5|              accountability in law enforcement in the Commonwealth," commonly referred to the Police Reform Bill.
   6|              These events continue to have an effect on our department as it relates to recruiting, retention, and morale.
   7|              Although faced with these challenges, the department remains committed to being pro-active, and strives to
   8|              make the Community safe for all.

  11|              town and region. A plumber by trade, but he was definitely considered a "jack of all trades." Off. Letarte
  12|              was one of the most dedicated individuals that I have been associated with. Off. Letarte would not hesitate

   1|                In 2021 there has been a 25% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Persons, with Aggravated and Simple
   2|                Assaults accounting for the majority of the incidents with 32 out of 52 total reported incidents. These
   3|                numbers appear to have a direct correlation to the Domestic Violence calls that officers have responded to
   4|               in 2021.
   5|                In 2021 there has been a 28% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Property. Although we have
   6|                observed a decrease in property crimes, we have observed an increase in Breaking and Entering into
   7|               residences and motor vehicles in the last quarter of the year.
   8|              The department continues to participate in annual in-service training. In addition several officers have
   9|              attended specialized training in various areas of Law Enforcement. Two areas that the department focused
  10|              on 2021 was empathy training, and wellbeing training for our members. In the spring the department
  11|              participated in "Effective Leadership through Emotional Intelligence, Empathetics." In the fall of 2021, Off
  12|              J. Mathieu and Off. B: McNamara provided training in "Mental Armor: Resilience and Wellbeing Skills for
  13|              First Responders." It is our belief that these types of training events are important for long and rewarding
  14|              careers for our members.
  15|              Our traffic officer, Off. J. Lachance, has competed the Advanced Crash Investigation course, which
  16|              provides the department with a certified Accident Reconstruction Investigator. Off. Lachance has also been
  17|              assigned to the CEMLEC Recon Team, which is the Central MA regional accident reconstruction team.
  18|              The department conducts annual Use of Force training, which includes de-escalation tactics. In 2021
  19|              officers were involved in nine (9) use of force incidents, as compared to 10 in 2020. In each Use of Force
  20|              incident de-escalation tactics, including the use of verbal tactics, were first utilized prior to any Use of Force Use or Force
  21|              application. Officers continue to strive to de-escalate confrontations prior to any application of Use of
  22|              Force.
  23|              Sgt. S. Lrate, Oft L. Melnicki, and Public Satety Coordinator A. Moore completed training for RADkids,
  24|              self- defense program for children. They will be hosting various community based trainings coming up ir
  25|              the spring of 2022. They are also certified RAD instructors, which is a self-defense program for women.
  26|              RAD classes will also be held throughout 2022.

   3|                                                     ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   4|            The Zoning Board of Appeals heard several petitions during calendar year 2021. There were several
   5|            applicants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations. The Zoning Board
   6|            meetings are held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month at Town Hall at 7:00 PM or ZOOM as
   7|            an alternative on an "as needed" basis.
   8|            The Zoning Board consists of the following members: Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice-
   9|            Chairman, Members: Hans Wentrup, David Blatt, Patrick Callahan and Anthony Nicastro, Associate
  10|            member. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  11|            The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  12|            1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-
  13|            laws.
  14|            2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  15|            3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General
  16|            Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit of
  17|            enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency,
  18|            abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  19|            4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws,
  20|            Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the
  21|            Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can by law
  22|            circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  23|            The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
  24|            Avenue, Lunenburg. Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00
  25|            PM Tuesday and Thursday, closed Friday.
  26|            If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421
  27|            during regular business hours. Zoning Board applications are available in the office and online at
  28|            www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

   1|                                                    EDUCATION
   2|                                                                          98

===PAGE 100=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|             and School Department to problem-solve in order to better meet the educational needs of the children of
   2|              Lunenburg.
   3|              Throughout the pandemic, the district has remained committed to making progress as outlined in on our
   4|              Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with our Mission and Vision:
   5|              The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and
   6|              passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship,
   7|              and rewarding lives.
   8|              The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of
   9|              attending to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered
  10|              instruction for deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills
  11|              necessary for continuous learning for all.
  12|              The Strategy is a living document which is reviewed annually and revised as needed. Annually in August,
  13|              the District Leadership Team reviews the Strategy, its objectives and action plans. The Strategy objectives
  14|              for the next three to four years are:
  15|              Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered and inclusive
  16|              by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work for
  17|              transforming practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning and
  18|              adapting practice to meet the needs of all students.
  19|              Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous learning in a
  20|              climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values.
  21|              Communication & Operations: To improve operational efficiencies, including communication, in order to
  22|              increase the organization's capacity to execute the district's mission and vision.
  23|              Equity, Access, Achievement & Opportunity for All Students: To ensure all students are provided equal
  24|              access to a high-quality education, that is collaborative, interactive, and relevant with an appropriate leve!
  25|              of support and challenge so that they can become well-rounded, resilient, confident, and capable young
  26|              adults adequately prepared for future challenges.
  27|              In order to cultivate district-wide leadership in key areas identified in the Strategy for Continuous
  28|              Improvement, the district continues to create teams to lead our work. Over the last three years we
  29|              established and have maintained the Behavioral Health Advisory Council, Professional Development
  30|              Committee, and Equity Advisory Council. During the 2020-2021 school-year we strengthened our general
  31|              education academic referral process and created a behavioral health referral process. These teams review
  32|              student cases referred by staff or administration and determine which academic or social/emotional/
  33|              behavioral interventions may be needed. Intervention plans are developed, implemented and reviewed for
  34|              impact and progress. In the spring of 2021, K-8 curriculum coordinator positions were created to assist
  35|              teachers in a specific content area and serve as teacher leaders in curriculum development, selection of
  36|                                                                          99

===PAGE 101=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|             at the T.C. Passios building before school for five days to be rapid tested for COVID. Students could then
   2|             sately report to school as long as results were negative. This helped us keep more students in school for in-
   3|             person learning.
   4|             For the 2020-2021 school-year, the district procured online assessment platforms knowing there would be
   5|             no available MCAS data. The district began administering these standardized assessments in October 2020
   6|             in grades K-5 and has continued to administer these assessments in the 2021-2022 school-year expanding
   7|             administration to include grades 6-8. These assessments provide the schools valuable information which
   8|             allow us to determine if our students are working on grade-level and develop targeted intervention for
   9|             individual and small groups of students who are not working on grade-level. Teachers continue to learn
  10|             how to utilize these data to inform classroom instruction.
  11|             Lunenburg Public Schools was accepted to participate in several of the Department of Elementary and
  12|             Secondary Education's academy programs. We are in the Social Emotional Learning (SEL) Academy,
  13|             Literacy Academy, and Selecting High Quality Math Materials Network. We have also sought and been
  14|             awarded just shy of $303,000 in competitive grants including, summer school grants, after school grants,
  15|             technology grants and school health services grants. These grants allow us to provide critical supports for
  16|             students.
  17|             Behavioral health support for students was an identified need prior to the pandemic, but now is a more
  18|             critical need exacerbated by the pandemic. We developed a structure for tiered supports including piloting a
  19|            • Tier 1 social emotional curriculum in all grades (K-12); and hiring additional guidance counselors to support
  20|             Tiers 1 and 2, and licensed social workers and adjustment counselors to provide Tier 3 supports and
  21|             families. We continued to provide Interface to the community. Interface is a behavioral health referral
  22|             service available to all residents of Lunenburg at no cost. In 2021-2022 the district added Care Solace, an
  23|             online resource with live 24/7 concierge meant to assist families and individuals in finding local mental
  24|             health support. Care Solace is a tool for school staff and families to connect with community-based mental
  25|             healthcare resources and providers. The vast database of mental health resources includes vetted local
  26|             therapists and programs.
  27|             Professional development for staff continues to be a priority as we continually strive for improvement. We
  28|             have continued our inter-district collaboration with North Middlesex Regional School District by holding an
  29|             annual joint conference each November. The Lunenburg Professional Development Committee will
  30|             continue to plan these days with the hope of collaborating with other districts as well.
  31|             For the 2020-2021 school-year, the district was able to restore a district-wide director-level curriculum
  32|             position which had been cut in 2012. This position is essential to provide supervision of and assistance to
  33|             teachers in a review of the curriculum, selection of quality curriculum materials, as well as organizing high
  34|             quality PD. Due to COVID-19, the 2020-2021 school-year was reduced from 180 days to 170 days,
  35|             therefore, making adjustments to pacing, prioritization, and sequencing of the curriculum necessary. As
  36|             MCAS was not administered in Spring 2020 due to school closure, the district procured an online
  37|             standardized assessment tool in order to have the necessary data to measure student growth and
  38|             performance, and to determine which

   1|            upgraded custodial equipment tor disintection, prepared supplies for the teaching environment such as
   2|            additional disinfectant spray bottles and microfiber wipers as well as using robust cleaning protocols to add
   3|            additional disinfection periods during and after the school day. Additionally the Facilities team provided
   4|             COVID-19 assistance with the distribution of masks and kits and testing operations as needed. Indoor air
   5|             quality assessment and regular changing of air filters were completed and measures taken to confirm that the
   6|             ventilation systems were performing well against the original design.
   7|             Security Upgrades
   8|             As part of ALICE safety training held on school campuses each school year, modifications were made on the
   9|             Primary and Turkey Hill schools' door hardware. This was needed to provide means to lock classroom and
  10|             office doors in the event of a lockdown other emergency requiring the locking of doors. Work was provided
  11|             by Oak Security.
  12|             Fire Alarm Upgrades
  13|             Under a State grant for $75,000 the fire alarm devices and main fire alarm panel at the Primary School were
  14|             replaced. This allowed for the new smoke and CO2 detectors to be replaced in every classroom, offices,
  15|             mechanical and storage rooms and created a more consistent and reliable fire alarm signal. Work was provide
  16|             by Norel Fire Services.
  17|             Water Testing
  18|             After completing 3 years of district-wide water testing for lead and copper in the drinking water at all schools
  19|             in June 2020, lead was detected in a number of sources at Turkey Hill and the TC Passios building. The
  20|             source of the lead is believed to be solder from joining copper pipes. The teaching staff, parents and students
  21|             have been made aware of the need to flush water sources at the start of the school day. A fixture replacement
  22|             program was implemented to remove and replace fixtures as required. Follow up testing occurred at all
  23|             schools in June 2021. In the continued effort to improve water quality within the schools the Facilities
  24|             department has started a State of Massachusetts grant application to replace existing drinking water stations
  25|             under the SWIG program or School Water Improvement Grant. If approved the grant will allow up to $3000
  26|             per replacement cost per water station.
  27|             Energy Savings
  28|             As a Green Community Grant participant since 2013, the Town has benefited from various awards allowing
  29|             upgraded energy efficient, Energy Star rated appliances at Turkey Hill Elementary at a cost of $15,092. A
  30|             LED lighting conversion for Lunenburg Middle/High School was completed in February 2021 with
  31|             $2,780.12. A replacement domestic hot water heater for the Primary School was complete by April 2021 with
  32|             $9,900 in green community grant funds. The Facilities Department has applied for the next round of grant
  33|             funding and will look to continue the effort to reduce cost and environmental impact.
  34|             Capital Program
  35|             The FY 22 Capital program received funding to begin air conditioning design for Lunenburg Primary School.
  36|             The subsequent assessment, preliminary design and cost estimate projected a cost of $232,738.31 to provide
  37|             necessary electrical wiring and equipment to provide air conditioning to the school. The assessment and
  38|             preliminary design was awarded to DiGiorgio Associates for $10,000.
  39|             Respectfully Submitted,
  40|             John C. Murphy, Director of Facilities & Grounds

   1|             indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate
   2|             measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon an age appropriate transition
   3|            assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably enable the student to meet
   4|            those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs. Lunenburg
   5|             was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in compliance.
   6|             Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. This indicator measures the % of
   7|            students with IEPs who exited high school during the 2018-2019 school year and self-reported post-school
   8|             engagement in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through
   9|             three measurements:
  10|             • Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  11|             • Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high
  12|                 school.
  13|             • Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training
  14|                 program; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high
  15|                 school.
  16|             Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-
  17|             secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2018-2019 school year, state targets for the
  18|             three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 50.2%; Measurement B: 79.6%; and
  19|             Measurement C: 87.3%. Lunenburg rates are as follows (from reporting year 2018-19): Measurement A =
  20|             50%, Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C= 75%.

  31|             works together with parents to provide these varied services. Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to
  32|             develop challenging and focused educational programs for children. We remain committed to the Lunenburg

===PAGE 109=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update
   2|             Lunenburg Public Schools returned to a full learning model for the 2021-2022 school year. Students are
   3|             invested in learning, and teachers are working diligently to evaluate student progress, identify learning gaps,
   4|             make adjustments to practice, and provide scaffolds accordingly.
   5|             We have implemented structured common planning time for teachers in grade level content areas so that
   6|             teachers may unpack and plan for units of study, align to standards, and look at data and student work. Our
   7|             district literacy specialist and our math instructional specialist meet with teachers during common planning
   8|             time to support curriculum development and help foster student growth. High school department heads
   9|             structure department meeting time to facilitate the necessary curriculum work needed to move each
  10|             department forward and improve learning for students.
  11|             Students in grades K-8 completed the fall and winter Ready diagnostic assessment in both reading and math,
  12|             and teachers are participating in data cycles to analyze data, plan for targeted support, and adjust instruction.
  13|             Personalized learning platforms through iReady provide differentiated instruction, systematic practice, and
  14|             scaffolded feedback in both reading and math.
  15|             The Lunenburg Public School District is participating in the Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS)
  16|             Literacy Academy and Social Emotional Learning Academy through the Department of Elementary and
  17|             Secondary Education (DESE). The Literacy Academy provides coaching sessions and technical assistance in
  18|             specific grade levels for literacy, including a focus on understanding the science of reading and standards-
  19|             based lesson planning. Both Academies assist our district with creating multi-tiered systems of support with a
  20|             focus on tier I, II, and Ill in academics and social emotional learning and behavioral health.
  21|             A K-5 math council including grade level teachers, special services teachers, EL teachers, and administrators,
  22|             has been working with DESE's Evaluating and Selecting High-Quality Materials Network to review and
  23|             select a core math curriculum. We are planning to begin a field test and pilot of several programs beginning
  24|             in February and make a final decision regarding purchase this spring.
  25|             Math teachers in grades K-5 have been working with a math consultant and have garnered new strategies for
  26|             mathematical discourse routines. Twenty staff members from throughout the district, including guidance
  27|             counselors, social workers, and teachers were trained and have been implementing the Trails to Wellness
  28|             curriculum, which is an evidence based social emotional wellness program rooted in cognitive behavioral
  29|             ideology.
  30|             Our Professional Development Committee continues to check-in with the district to evaluate needs and
  31|             prepare for upcoming professional learning including future book studies and workshops. Our administrative
  32|             team and our curriculum leaders are working with a consultant on workshops pertaining to diversity, equity,
  33|             and inclusion and plan to proceed with this work to include the entire district staff. The team is also
  34|             participating in a book study based on the book Caste, by Isabel Wilkerson, which takes a close look at social
  35|             constructs in the United States. Our team is studying these constructs in connection with our school system
  36|             and identifying ways we can improve student inclusion and experiences in Lunenburg.
  37|                                                                         108

  13|             Since May of 2020, when the United States Department of Agriculture's (USDA) Food and Nutrition Service
  14|             (FNS) approved a request by the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE) to allow school
  15|             food authorities flexibility while continuing to feed children for free. Therefore, we can ensure all students
  16|             have access to the nutrition they need during the school day. Our program like many others in the United
  17|             States continues to struggle with the supply chain breakdown. Our orders are late or shorted sometimes while
  18|             the numbers for breakfast and lunch are growing.
  19|             Lunenburg School Lunch Program is participating in Harvest of the Month. Massachusetts Harvest of the
  20|             Month program promotes a different Massachusetts-produced food each month in cafeterias and meal sites
  21|             across the state. The goal is to encourage healthy food choices by increasing students' exposure to seasonal
  22|             foods while supporting local farmers. Harvest of the Month is a great opportunity to celebrate local foods,
  23|             experiment with new recipes and lead the way in making farm-fresh foods available to students and staff.
  24|             It also provides wonderful opportunities for school food and nutrition staff, educators, school administrators,
  25|             family members and students to collaborate and celebrate a commitment to serving locally grown foods within
  26|             the community.
  27|             We want to wish Sarah Romano and Patricia Pichnarcik health and happiness in their next adventures as both
  28|             have retired from Lunenburg Middle-High School. We have some new faces in each of the cateterias:
  29|              Marlene Aubuchon-Hoaglund, Vanessa Cole, Jennifer Cotoni, Jessica McCarthy and Krystal Morini have
  30|             joined our team. Each bring their own special set of skills with them.

===PAGE 111=== (ocr, 38 lines)
   1|             Our professional development has centered on literacy instruction and Social Emotional Learning this year.
   2|             We have provided the reading, writing, and phonics resources of the Lucy Calkins Units of Study to our
   3|            instructional staff at all grade levels. In order to provide staff collaboration time to work with new curricular
   4|             resources we added a Common Planning Time by adjusting our building schedule and reallocating existing
   5|             support staff. These two actions have allowed for staff to participate in professional development focused on
   6|             our literacy and mathematics instruction. We have been able to access consultants as well district curriculum
   7|             coordinators so that teachers have an opportunity as a whole grade level to develop and provide evidence
   8|             based instruction, common learning experiences and share effective teaching strategies to support student
   9|             academic growth.
  10|              Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. We continue to
  11|             implement the Responsive Classroom model in all of our classrooms. Positive Behavioral Interventions and
  12|             Supports (PBIS) is an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data,
  13|             systems, and practices affecting student outcomes every day. We have adopted a new Social Emotional
  14|             resource, Trails to Wellness which is a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional
  15|             learning. It is designed to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and regulate their
  16|             emotions.
  17|             The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure
  18|             that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in the
  19|             fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These
  20|             common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students.
  21|             Struggling students are identified based on assessment data for access to our current Multi-Tiered Support
  22|             System (MTSS) in Reading, Math, Speech, Occupational and Physical Therapy
  23|             The Primary School Council meets each month to discuss school related concerns. Serving as an advisory
  24|             group to the principal, the council is charged with reviewing the school building budget and developing the
  25|             school improvement plan.
  26|             Members of the Council for the 2021-2022 school year are:
  27|             Principal: Chad Adams Assistant Principal: Kevin White
  28|             Teachers: Sarah Curley, Kara Slattery, Nancy Murray, Patti Kilcommins
  29|             Parents: Meghan Arnold, Shaunna Baril
  30|             School Committee Representative: Brian Lehtinen
  31|             We are honored and humbled to be afforded the opportunity to work with our highly dedicated district wide
  32|             colleagues and a caring community in order to provide the best possible educational opportunities for our
  33|             youngest students.
  34|             For more information about the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website:
  35|             https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/primary/

  37|             Chad Adams, Principal

   1|            hybrid model. The majority of students returned to school, fully masked. Stringent guidelines remained in
   2|            place: 6 feet of space when possible, masks even during recess time, desks and high touch surfaces frequently
   3|            wiped down, socially distanced lunchrooms, and scheduled mask and water breaks outside. We continue to
   4|            have hand sanitizer throughout the building and students use water filling stations for bottles, not water
   5|            bubblers. The number of students who could take the bus was limited, causing a significant increase in drop-
   6|            offs and pick-ups.
   7|            We canceled the fifth grade trip to Nature's Classroom in Beckett that is usually slated for October; however,
   8|            in May we invited Nature's Classroom staff to our school. They conducted several days of outdoor activities
   9|            with fifth graders.
  10|             We participated in the MCAS tests in April and May, per DESE's directive. All three tests (Math, ELA, and
  11|            science) were shortened to one session instead of two per subject. Remote students participated remotely,
  12|            using the Google Meet as a way to monitor the fidelity of the test taking.
  13|            In June the fifth grade celebrated their "completion ceremonies". Local public access television was kind
  14|            enough to record the events so parents could enjoy the assemblies via video.
  15|             All Chromebooks were collected during the last school week to be cleaned and repaired. Each grade level
  16|            held a field day outside for students that included fun activities, games, and picnic lunches. Fully remote
  17|            students were invited to join.
  18|            In late August we reopened to a full in-person model. There is no longer the option for a remote or hybrid
  19|            schedule. Students and staff are still obliged to wear masks inside the building, but they can be removed while
  20|             outside, including recess. We established six lunchtimes so all students can eat in the cafeteria while
  21|             maintaining six feet of distance. We also established three feet of space between desks to lessen the possibility
  22|             of "close contacts" in the classroom.
  23|             We made the decision to pilot a "no homework" policy this year. This is based on our experience last year,
  24|             during the COVID remote time, when there was no homework. Our intention is that students do their best
  25|             work in school and use after school as an opportunity to be social with friends, take on a new sport or hobby,
  26|            . and not feel stressed about more worksheets or academic demands. We do encourage all students to spend
  27|            time each evening enjoying independent reading. We recognize that there is inequity for students who go
  28|             home to a variety of situations some of which are more or less conducive to homework, and have chosen to
  29|             mitigate that obstacle. We will review our test scores at year's end to make a decision regarding next year.
  30|             Turkey Hill Elementary School currently has 363 students. There are six sections of grade 3, with a total of
  31|             134 students. There are five sections of grades 4 and grades 5, with totals of 113 students and 116 students
  32|            respectively. There are 26 teachers, 100% of whom are licensed in their assignment area. Our special areas
  33|            include physical education, art, music, technology/library and social emotional learning. We are fortunate to
  34|            have a full time assistant principal, two full time nurses, a psychologist, BCBA, and a full time adjustment
  35|             counselor/social worker.
  36|                                                                         112

   2|            learning gaps. Ready is an excellent way to document student progress in reading and math and identify

   4|            Our literacy specialist, interventionist, and two tutors work together to identify students with significant gaps
   5|            in their grade level skills and provide 8-10 weeks of dedicated instruction. They use LLI (Language
   6|            Literacy Intervention) for reading and a variety of supporting materials for math.
   7|            We are happy that all of the students are back in the building, despite the health challenges. We need to be
   8|            together, physically, as a community of learners, to connect and support each other. The students are
   9|            amazing in their resilience and capacity to handle all of the restrictions with grace. Looking forward to the
  10|            promise of 2022 and hopefully a more normalized environment.

===PAGE 116=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                          Lunenburg Middle School
   2|             Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support
   3|             students' social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated,
   4|             creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest
   5|             communication. The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the
   6|             philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a
   7|             means by which all students become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in
   8|             high school and beyond.
   9|              Lunenburg Middle School is in its sixth year as a co-located middle -high school serving grades six through
  10|             eight. Effective, student centered middle schools embrace interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable
  11|             teachers to work with smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and
  12|             engage students in their preferred learning style, making them more confident and successful.
  13|              Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of
  14|              different interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities.
  15|              One of our goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue
  16|             exploring as they move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for
  17|             students that foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond
  18|              or where their passion may lead them to.
  19|                                                     Return to full in person instruction
  20|             The global pandemic that began in March of 2020 has disrupted and interfered with the method of
  21|             instruction our school community has become accustomed to. Since that time, we have participated in fully
  22|             remote learning, hybrid model of learning, and in August of 2021, we returned to a full in person learning.
  23|             The pandemic took a toll on students, teachers and families, both academic and socially and emotionally.
  24|             Lunenburg Middle school has prepared and planned for the full in person return, and has placed personnel
  25|             and programs in place to ensure students are serviced in all components of their well-being.
  26|              Lunenburg Middle School now has a full time social worker to work with students who may experience an
  27|             increased level of anxiety in returning to the building full time. The social worker also is able to run small
  28|              groups for students to develop executive function skills, strategies for managing anxiety and stress and help
  29|              develop social skills. Our social worker has also done an outstanding job with managing and facilitating
  30|              crisis management and assessing student's well-being and aligning out of school services for students and
  31|             families.
  32|                                                               Athletics and clubs
  33|              In our return to a full in person model of learning we also returned to extracurricular activities and athletics
  34|              at LMS. In the fall, we successfully participated in middle school Field Hockey and Boys and Girls Cross
  35|              Country and this winter we returned middle school basketball after many years of it being eliminated.
  36|                                                                         115

   6|             2021 Spring MCAS testing at LMS was challenging logistically this year as many students took the test
   7|            remotely from home. MCAS scores for this year were more of a diagnostic to determine if and how much
   8|            learning loss students suffered during the pandemic. The scores above show the percentage of students who
   9|             scored in the " meeting or Exceeding" category and then the column next to it shows or scores in comparison
  10|             with the state scores of schools who service similar grade levels. As you can see, even during the pandemic
  11|             year, LMS scores are above the state percentage in six out of the seven areas tested. We hope to see continued
  12|            improvement in our MCAS scores in 2022 as it will reflect a full year of in person learning and targeting
  13|             student learning gaps.
  14|             Science Olympiad, Newspaper Club, Yearbook Club and middle school Student Government return as
  15|             favorite activities for students to engage in. Student Government held a "Movie Night" for middle school
  16|             students after school outside in the recess yard.
  17|             Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that ensure
  18|             student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning even during this unprecedented time of a global
  19|             pandemic. The key components to our success continue to be our talented faculty committed to making a
  20|            positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and learning, and most of
  21|            all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning during this difficult time. Together we
  22|             continue to make the difference in the lives of students.

   1|             instruction our school community has become accustomed to. Since that time, we have participated in fully
   2|             remote learning, hybrid model of learning, and in August of 2021, we returned to a full in person learning
   3|             model. The pandemic took a toll on students, teachers and families, both academically and socially and
   4|             emotionally. Lunenburg High school has prepared and planned for the full in person return, and has placed
   5|              personnel and programs in place to ensure students are serviced in all components of their well-being.
   6|             LHS added four positions to its faculty in order to support students returning from a global pandemic. Two
   7|             assistant principals were put in place to support the interim LMHS principal. The assistant principals have
   8|              been integral in establishing a student centered culture, establishing routines and structures for students to
   9|             feel safe and supported, and supporting the faculty so they are able to devote their time and energy on
  10|              student learning.
  11|              LHS has also added an additional Guidance counselor to assist and support students back to traditional high
  12|             school schedule. This position has benefited our school community by reinventing the guidance suite to be
  13|              a student centered area where kids can seek out support or investigate college and careers opportunities.
  14|              The guidance counselor position also has been helpful in identifying students who are struggling with stress
  15|              or anxiety or are in need of other supports.
  16|              LHS added a math teacher to provide additional sections of mathematic courses and reduce the number of
  17|              students in sections where some students traditionally struggle. The reduction in teacher/ student ratio will
  18|              allow teacher to provide a more personalized, individualized instruction. We also were able to add a
  19|              Statistics course and a Math Skills Building course.
  20|              The additions to the faculty at Lunenburg High School have allowed students a more varied options for
  21|              course selection, provided support and structure for both students and staff to focus on instruction and
  22|             learning. LHS looks forward to reporting out on the difference in student learning these positions have
  23|              made.

   1|                          HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
   2|            Homecoming weekend was a wonderful event with beautiful weather and well attended by students,
   3|            families and alumni. The parade was a huge success with the collection of candy the biggest hit with the
   4|            children in town. Many community members noticed the care and time that were placed into the floats at
   5|            each grade level. The Blue Knights played well in the football game and we always enjoy watching the
   6|            LMS Marching Band perform.
   7|                                    Lunenburg High School Athletic Accomplishments
   8|            The LHS Girls Cross Country Team won the MIAA Division 3B Championship and the Boys and Girls
   9|            Cross Country and Boys Soccer finished their seasons as league Champions.
  10|            All of Lunenburg High School's sports teams qualified for the new MIAA State Tournament Playoff
  11|            system. Seven teams were recognized as MIAA Team Academic Excellence Awards. Six Teams were gold
  12|            level recipients, with a 3.0 team GPA, and one team earned a silver level, with a team GPA of 2.5 or higher:
  13|            48% of the High School students participated in fall athletics as well as 20% of the Middle school students;
  14|            That's34% of the total student body at LMHS participated in Fall athletics. Lunenburg High School was
  15|            able to offer 8 different sports and 16 levels (Varsity, Junior Varsity, and Middle School)
  16|            Four of our high school teams were recognized by the MIAA with a State Sportsmanship Awards. The team
  17|            recognized were Football, Field Hockey and both Boys and Girls Soccer.
  18|            Blue Knights Football was the only team to be recognized out of 277 teams in the state. LHS Girls' Soccer
  19|            was the only team to be recognized out of 331 teams in the state. LHS Field Hockey was 1 of 2 teams out of
  20|            200 to be recognized with the Sportsmanship Award. Boys' Soccer was 1 of 4 teams out of 332 in the state
  21|            to be recognized for the MIAA Sportsmanship Award.
  22|            Additionally, the MIAA noted, this is the first time a single high school received four Sportsmanship awards
  23|            in a single season. The Football team was also recognized by the Central Mass Football Officials
  24|            Sportsmanship Award. Girls Indoor and Outdoor Track Coach Steve Kyajohnian was inducted into the
  25|            Massachusetts State Track Coaches Association Hall of Fame, an incredible honor
  26|            Girls Basketball Coach Frank Greene was awarded the Massachusetts Basketball Coaching Award and
  27|            MIAA Coach of the Year.
  28|                                                               CURRICULUM
  29|             Teachers and administrators continue to refine, revise and create curriculum that is aligned to the state
  30|            frameworks and is rigorous and challenging. This year's focus is to work in vertical content area teams to
  31|            ensure a smooth transition for students as they advance grade by grade. This process also ensures that
  32|            students will have and build upon the educational skills that they will need to be successful in future grades.
  33|            Scope and sequence maps have been created in order to assist in this grade level curriculum mapping.
  34|            Lunenburg High School continues to provide a high quality education s that ensures student success and
  35|            promotes a passion for lifelong learning even during this unprecedented time of a global pandemic. The key.
  36|             components to our success continue to be our talented faculty, our parents and our community members
  37|            who support our teaching and learning. Most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment
  38|            to learning during this difficult time. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  39|            Respectfully Submitted, Tim Santry, LMHS Principal

   5|                    Vocational Technical

===PAGE 123=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|            deep-rooted knowledge of how students learn best - in-person. School leaders worked tirelessly to develop a schedule
   2|            that would bring as many students back into the building as possible, safely, while attempting to balance academic and
   3|            vocational in-person learning time. Instructors were provided with additional technology supports and added
   4|            professional development days, to write and create curriculum that could be delivered remotely. This, in itself, would
   5|             be a challenge. How do you teach a student how to prepare and present a meal, or demonstrate fine-motor skills
   6|            required to give a haircut, in a virtual learning environment? How do you assess a student's understanding of
   7|            residential electrical wiring, without having the ability to observe and manipulate his/her work? How can you teach
   8|            students to safely restrain an animal, with no pet interaction? I am proud to say that the Monty Tech instructors rose to
   9|            this incredible challenge, and for much of the 2020-2021 school year, delivered an effective educational program,
  10|            using the resources that were available to them.
  11|            While face-to-face instruction was provided, that, too, looked and felt different to students and staff. On those days
  12|             when students were scheduled to attend in-person lessons, they found things had changed. Traffic patterns were
  13|            rerouted, and signage on floors, hallways and doors reminded them to wear a mask and maintain distance. Seating on
  14|             busses and in the cafeteria was assigned and closely monitored. A place that had always encouraged connection with
  15|            fellow classmates was now insisting on distance. Though it may have been uncomfortable for both students and staff,
  16|            the school wide adherence to these new rules seemed to be a quiet demonstration of their understanding. And on May
  17|             3, 2021, their compliance was rewarded - Monty Tech was allowed to return to full, in-person learning. With just
  18|             over a month remaining in the 2020-2021 school year, students and staff welcomed the change, anxious for the halls
  19|            and classrooms to return to a more normal capacity. While masks, social distancing and numerous hand sanitizing
  20|            stations were still present, the return of students into the building was a welcome sight for all.
  21|            In 2020-2021, it was disappointing that so many traditional annual events had to be cancelled, including VIP, Tour
  22|            Day, College Fair, and Career Awareness Night; however, where possible, the district continued to operate at a high
  23|            level. Examples include:
  24|            To help our faculty and staff return to work, daycare issues had to be addressed, and, as a result, an important
  25|            relationship with the Boys and Girls Club of Fitchburg and Leominster developed. The club provided daycare and
  26|            homework help, Monday through Friday, to school age children of Monty Tech faculty and staff.
  27|            Thanks to more than $670,000 in competitive grant awards, Monty Tech was able to renovate and expand the
  28|             welding/metal fabrication shop and retrofit one science lab. The school launched an all new evening training program
  29|            that provided no-cost training and job placement to qualified participants. With grant funds, we also established a
  30|             Safe and Supportive Schools Team to review school policies and procedures and target those in need of improvement,
  31|            with an overarching goal of providing all students with safe and appropriate learning environments.
  32|             In an effort to build connections with our school supporters and to "show off" the talents of our students, we held our
  33|            first Holiday Sale, which quickly sold out! More than 400 charcuterie trays, designed and handcrafted by our
  34|            Cabinetmaking students were sold, and all proceeds went to the Class of 2021 Scholarship Fund.
  35|            The 2020-2021 school year was unexpected, challenging, and at times, disappointing, yet there were so many
  36|             moments to be proud of. I remain humbled and honored to lead such an outstanding organization, and thank you for
  37|             your continued support.
  38|            Respectfully submitted, Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director

   6|             Class of 2021 Awards
   7|             Members of the Class of 2021 were awarded approximately $200,000 in scholarships, tools, and equipment. The
   8|             Monty Tech Foundation generously provided $35,500 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts
   9|             of $250 to $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $3,250 to the Practical Nursing graduates. Once again, local and
  10|             state organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
  11|             graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates are
  12|             grateful for this support.
  13|             Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher education.
  14|             Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articulation agreements with
  15|             public and private colleges across the country. Approximately 36% of the graduating class of 2021 reported plans to
  16|             enroll at a 4-ear college/
  17|            university upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will save both
  18|             time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  19|              Financial Report
  20|             In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2020-2021, a great deal of effort was put forth by the
  21|             School Committee, administration, and staff. The final fiscal year 2020-2021 Educational Plan totaled $28,814,865
  22|             which represents a 0.19% increase over the 2019-2020 Educational Plan. The District's FY21 budget only exceeds the
  23|             minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by $899,640 or 3.5%.
  24|             The District was audited in December 2021 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson,
  25|             Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and expects to receive a good report.
  26|              Grants and Contracts
  27|             Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many educational and
  28|             social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2021, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
  29|             $2,037,076. Programs funded by these grants include: Coronavirus Prevention and Relief, Improving Teacher
  30|             Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education,
  31|             Vocational Capital Equipment initiatives, Student Support and Academic Enrichment and Marine Corp Junior Reserve
  32|             Officer Training.

  24|            to utilize e-learning program options to keep the remaining students on track and engaged during the hybrid

   8|             working side-by-side with some of the area's finest chefs. But 2020-2021 was a different year. Due to COVID, and

   3|            in Athol, replacing a concrete walk at Hubbardston Council on Aging, and repairing a granite wall at the Fay Club in
   4|            Fitchburg. A creative solution to restrictive in-person guidelines, Monty Tech Masonry students and instructors
   5|             designed, placed and finished a 24 'x 24' concrete slab outside, creating an outdoor classroom so that students could
   6|            continue to work on important projects, yet remain socially distant and safe. Students continued to maintain the OSHA
   7|            silica standard, in a program that operates in compliance with all trade regulations. Three Seniors earned co-op

   9|            program. One Senior won a silver medal at the SkillsUSA State competition, and all juniors completed the industry-
  10|            standard HotWorks Certification. (Total student enrollment: 45)
  11|            Plumbing: Like programs across the school, the Monty Tech Plumbing program struggled with delivering hands-on
  12|            lessons that would rival their in-person instruction. However, talented instructors in the school's popular shop worked
  13|             diligently to rewrite curriculum and design lessons that could be adapted. With a focus on theory and technical
  14|            knowledge, instructors aimed to embed certification opportunities where possible. To that end, students in grades 10,
  15|            11 and 12 successfully completed their HotWorks safety training and earned that important credential. Freshmen also
  16|            completed the 10-hour OSHA general industry safety training, while Sophomore students completed the 10-hour
  17|             OSHA Construction safety training and certification. Students and instructors collaborated with other trades to
  18|             complete two single family house building projects, both located in Ashburnham. Students and instructors also began
  19|            rough plumbing for the Habitat for Humanity single family home in Athol. Back on campus, students replaced three

  23|            enrollment: 67)
  24|            Veterinary Science: 2020-2021 was the 4th year for the school's newest vocational training program - Veterinary
  25|            Science. Unfortunately, due to COVID restrictions, traffic in the community clinic was somewhat limited. The clinic
  26|             did, though, provide low cost veterinary care to 421 families with 635 pets within our district. 14 Seniors earned the
  27|            CVA (Certified Veterinary Assistant) certification; 20 Seniors achieved their Recover CPR Certification; 21 Juniors
  28|            achieved their Fear Free Certification; and 22 Freshman successfully completed OSHA 10-hour Healthcare training
  29|             and certification. Freshmen and Sophomores learned about the use of animals, anatomy, disease, laboratory skills and
  30|             client service. Junior and Senior lessons in pharmacology, surgical and nursing care, and anesthesiology proved to be
  31|            invaluable to the students working in the clinic, applying their knowledge with purpose alongside veterinarians and
  32|            veterinary technicians. (Total student enrollment: 85)
  33|            Welding/Metal Fabrication: The 2020-2021 school year brought some exciting news to the Welding/metal Fabrication
  34|            program. Thanks to a partnership developed while working on a Skills Capital Grant, the Monty Tech Welding/Metal
  35|            Fabrication program became the first pre-apprenticeship program in the school's history. This 5-year agreement with
  36|            the Plumbers & Pipefitters Union #4 will help create a meaningful pathway for students to enter into a registered

  41|             Fitchburg Riverfront Park, and fabricated and welded three clothing racks for the Town of Hubbardston Senior

  43|             D1.1 Structural Welding Code Qualification Test. All grade 9 students completed 10 Hour OSHA training. (Total
